As the main step of this test, both parties do an (RO-padded) Hadamard test on the combined gadget.

Protocol 10

(
{\mathsf{CoPhTest}}
)
.

Suppose the security parameter is
\kappa
and the gadget number is controlled by
L
.

Client holds a tuple of key pairs
K=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
a tuple of phase pairs
\Theta=(\Theta^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
.

Honest server holds:

\otimes_{i\in[0,L]}{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(i)},\Theta^{(i)})

1.

Both parties run
{\mathsf{Combine}}(K,\Theta;1^{\kappa})
. The client gets
K^{(combined)}
and
\Theta^{(combined)}
and the server gets
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(combined)},\Theta^{(combined)})
.

2.

The client chooses to run one of the following two randomly, and the honest server could use
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(combined)},\Theta^{(combined)})
to pass the tests:

•

{\mathsf{StdBTest}}(K^{(combined)})
.

•

{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}(K^{(combined)},\Theta^{(combined)};1^{\kappa})
.

Individual Phase Test

The individual phase test is defined as follows.

Protocol 11

(
{\mathsf{InPhTest}}
)
.

Suppose the security parameter is
\kappa
and the gadget number is
L
.

Client holds a tuple of key pairs
K=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
a tuple of phase pairs
\Theta=(\Theta^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
.

Honest server holds:

\otimes_{i\in[0,L]}{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(i)},\Theta^{(i)})

This protocol will only use the first gadget, which corresponds to
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(0)},\Theta^{(0)})
.

1.

With probability
\frac{1}{3}
each, the client executes the following with the server without telling the server which is the case:

•

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}(K^{(0)},\Theta^{(0)},0;1^{\kappa})
;

•

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}(K^{(0)},\Theta^{(0)},4;1^{\kappa})
;

•

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}(K^{(0)},\Theta^{(0)},1;1^{\kappa})
; Note “
{\mathsf{win}}
” or “
{\mathsf{lose}}
” are recorded as the score corresponding to the client’s checking result.

basis uniformity test

Finally we formalize the basis uniformity test as follows. The input gadgets are indexed by
1
to
L
. The client selects a random subset of index
I
from
[L]
, which represents the indices of the gadgets that will be used for the combine-and-test process; then it uses
{\mathsf{Combine}}
to combine these gadgets into a single gadget. Then:

1.

For the gadgets outside
I
, the client will ask the server to measure them in the standard basis.

2.

For the combined part, one of the following two is randomly selected:

•

A standard basis test on the combined gadget.

•

As the main step of this test, both parties execute a Hadamard test on this combined gadget.

Note that the main body of
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
is on the gadgets without phases (
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(\cdots)})
). But when we use the
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
in Protocol
2
the phases are already added. Thus we first formalize a version of
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
that additionally takes a tuple of phase information as parameters that does the following: it simply reveals the phases and allows the server to de-phase the gadgets and then calls the main body of
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
.

Protocol 12

(
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
starting from gadgets with phases)
.

Suppose the security parameter is
\kappa
and the gadget number is
L
.

Client holds a tuple of key pairs
\tilde{K}=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
and a tuple of phase pairs
(\Theta^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
.

Honest server holds:

{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(1)},\Theta^{(1)})\otimes{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(2)},\Theta^{(2)})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(L)},\Theta^{(L)})

(99)

1.

The client reveals
\Theta^{(1)},\Theta^{(2)}\cdots\Theta^{(L)}
and the server could remove the phases from (
99
) and get

{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(1)})\otimes{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(2)})\otimes\cdots{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(L)})

2.

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{BUTest}}(\tilde{K}^{(i)};1^{\kappa})
defined below.

Protocol 13

(
{\mathsf{BUTest}}
)
.

Suppose the security parameter is
\kappa
and the gadget number is
L
.

Client holds a tuple of key pairs
\tilde{K}=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
.

Honest server holds:

{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(1)})\otimes{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(2)})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(L)})

(100)

1.

The client samples a random subset of index
I\subseteq[L]
.

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{Combine}}(K,I;1^{\kappa})
. This combines the gadgets with superscripts in
I
into a single gadget. The client gets
K^{(combined)}
and the server gets
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(combined)})
. The gadgets with superscripts in
[L]-I
do not change.

2.

The client asks the server to measure all the gadgets excluding
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(combined)})
in the standard basis and send back the results (denoted as
rp^{(j)}
for each
j\in[L]-I
). The client checks
rp^{(j)}\in K^{(j)}
for each
j\in[L]-I
, and rejects if it’s not satisfied.

3.

The client chooses one of the following two randomly:

•

The client asks the server to measure
K^{(combined)}
in the computational basis and send back the result. The client checks the server’s response is within
K^{(combined)}
.

•

Both parties execute
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}(K^{(combined)};1^{\kappa})
on the combined gadget.

So far we have completed the formalization of our pre-RSPV protocol. Below we give its correctness, efficiency and verifiability, which corresponds to Definition
4.4
,
4.5
.

5.4
Properties of Our
{\mathsf{preRSPV}}
Protocol

Protocol
2
has the following properties.

Round type probability

The round type is chosen from
({\mathsf{test}},{\mathsf{quiz}},{\mathsf{comp}})
with probability
(\frac{4}{5},\frac{1}{10},\frac{1}{10})
correspondingly. Denote them as
p_{{\mathsf{test}}},p_{{\mathsf{quiz}}},p_{{\mathsf{comp}}}
.

Correctness

The protocol in the honest settings prepares the target state

\ket{+_{\theta^{(1)}}}\otimes\ket{+_{\theta^{(2)}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{+_{\theta^{(L)}}}

in the
{\mathsf{comp}}
round. The client gets
\theta^{(1)}\theta^{(2)}\cdots\theta^{(L)}\in_{r}\{0,1\cdots 7\}^{L}
. This is as required in Definition
4.4
.

Winning Probability

The winning probability in the quiz round in the honest setting is

{\text{OPT}}=\frac{1}{3}|\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{{\mathrm{i}}\pi/4}|^{2}=\frac{1}{3}\cos^{2}(\pi/8)=0.28451779686\cdots

where the first
1/3
comes from the fact that in the extra-biased Hadamard test, if the extra phase bias
\delta\in\{0,4\}
the protocol does not generate
{\mathsf{win}}/{\mathsf{lose}}
output.

Efficiency

The complexity of both parties is
O({\mathsf{poly}}(\kappa)|C|)
. Note that since we are working on the MBQC model where long-range interactions come with a cost, the complexity analysis needs to be careful. By analyzing the honest mapping we can confirm that the total complexity of honest behavior is
O({\mathsf{poly}}(\kappa)|C|)
even in the MBQC model.
19
19

19

One place that needs to be additionally careful is how to model the cost of random oracle queries. In our protocol there are constant number of queries where the input is a long string, whose length is linear to
L
. It’s reasonable to consider the cost of this action to be within
O({\mathsf{poly}}(\kappa)L)
.

Optimality of
OPT

Theorem 5.1
.

OPT
is optimal with error tolerance
(10^{-2000},10^{-220})
(as formalized in Definition
4.6
).

For the verifiability we have:

Verifiability

Theorem 5.2
.

Protocol
2
has verifiability with error tolerance
(10^{-2000},10^{-200})
(as formalized in Definition
4.5
).

We prove Theorem
5.1
and
5.2
in Section
13
.

6
Basic Notions and Analysis of Key-Pair Preparation and Standard Basis Test

In this section we develop tools for analyzing the part of Protocol
2
before the verifiable state preparation step.

•

First, in Section
6.1
we give symbols for registers that appear in the protocols. Then we get a clearer set-up for later proofs.

•

Then in Section
6.2
we will give some basic notions for characterizing the state’s properties. They include the
efficiently preparable
,
key checkable
and
claw-free
properties, which are basically reviews or re-formalizations of existing notions; and we define the
strong-claw-free
to characterize the uncorrelated claw-freeness of multiple key pairs. These notions will also be the basis of later proofs.

•

Then in Section
6.3
we analyze the first step of Protocol
2
, the parallel application of
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
. We will show the output state of this step satisfies the properties above.

•

Then in Section
6.4
we give the notion of
basis-honest form
, which means the server holds exactly one key from each key pair that the client holds. We also define the
branch
of basis-honest form formally, which will be useful in later proofs.

•

In Section
6.5
we analyze the properties of standard basis test (
{\mathsf{StdBTest}}
). We will show the ability of passing the standard basis test with high probability implies the state is approximately isometric to a basis-honest form via a server-side isometry.

•

Finally in Section
6.6
we prove some lemmas that will be used in later proofs.

Note that we will introduce the
Setup
to organize the properties of a state family.

6.1
Symbols for Different Registers

By the time of the completion of step
1
in Protocol
2
, the registers include:

•

The client-side key registers: denoted by
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{({\text{switch}})}),{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
,
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{(i)},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(i)})
.

When the security parameter is
\kappa
, the size of each
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}
register is
\kappa
.

For each
i\in\{{\text{switch}},[0,L]\}
, define
{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})
as
\{(x_{0},x_{1}):x_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa},x_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{\kappa},x_{0}\neq x_{1}\}
.

As a convention, we use
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}
to denote
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
.

•

The transcript registers hold the server’s response in the first step, which is denoted by
y^{({\text{switch}})}y^{(0)}\cdots y^{(L)}
in the protocol; denote the corresponding registers by
{\boldsymbol{Y}}=({\boldsymbol{Y}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{(0)},{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{(1)},\cdots{\boldsymbol{Y}}^{(L)})
.

•

The client-side phase registers: although these phases are not sampled and used in the protocol until the step (a) of the verifiable state preparation step, we could assume the client has already sampled them out in advance. These information is stored in registers
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=({\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
.
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{(i)}=({\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}^{(i)},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1}^{(i)})
. The domain of each
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
register is
\{0,1\cdots 7\}
.

•

The server’s registers. In general these registers are denoted by symbol
{\boldsymbol{S}}
; and we note there are many different registers appeared in different steps of the protocol. We will use superscripts to refer to these different registers. Especially, denote the registers that holds the
{\mathsf{gadget}}(K^{(i)})
in (
73
) in the honest setting as
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}
. Denote
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}=({\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
.

•

The random oracle registers that store the random oracle outputs for each input. The symbols are as used in Section
3.3
.

6.2
Basic Notions on Joint States

We give the following basic notions for characterizing the properties of a state.

Definition 6.1

(Efficiently preparable states, repeated)
.

We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is efficiently-preparable if there exists a polynomial time operator (which could include projections)
O
such that
\ket{\varphi}=O\ket{0}
.

Definition 6.2

(Key-checkable)
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{x}}
is a client-side register that holds a key. We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is key-checkable for
{\boldsymbol{x}}
if there exists an efficient server-side operation
20
20

20

In general this server-side operation is implemented with the help of transcript registers (which are the
{\boldsymbol{Y}}
registers).
that implements
\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{={\boldsymbol{x}}}
on
\ket{\varphi}
where
{\boldsymbol{S}}
is an arbitrary server-side register,
\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{={\boldsymbol{x}}}
denotes the projection onto the space that the content of
{\boldsymbol{S}}
is equal to the content of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

Suppose the client holds a tuple of keys in registers
{\boldsymbol{K}}
. We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is key-checkable for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
if for any single key register
{\boldsymbol{x}}
within
{\boldsymbol{K}}
,
\ket{\varphi}
is key-checkable for
{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

Then we review the notion of claw-freeness.

Definition 6.3

(Claw-free
[
12
]
)
.

Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
. We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
against adversary family
{\mathcal{F}}
if for any adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
, for an arbitrary server-side register
{\boldsymbol{S}}
,

|\Pi_{={\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}||{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}{\mathsf{Adv}}\ket{\varphi}|\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)

where
\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}_{={\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}||{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}}
is a projection onto the subspace that the content of
{\boldsymbol{S}}
is equal to the content of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}||{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}
.

We omit
{\mathcal{F}}
when it is taken to be the set of polynomial time server-side operations.

And we further generalize it to the multi key pair setting:

Definition 6.4

(Strongly claw-free)
.

Suppose the client holds a tuple of key pairs in register
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}
where
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{(i)},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(i)})
. We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is strongly-claw-free for any key pair in
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}
if for any
i\in D
,
\ket{\varphi}\odot(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})
is claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
, where
\ket{\varphi}\odot(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})
means, starting from
\ket{\varphi}
, the client sends all the keys except the
i
-th key pair to the server.

6.3
Analysis of the Key-pair Superposition Preparation Step

We have the following theorem which characterizes the properties of output states from the first step of Protocol
2
(a parallel
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
evaluation).

Theorem 6.1
.

For any polynomial time adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
, denote the output state of the first step of Protocol
2
as
\ket{\varphi^{1}}
. Then registers described in Section
6.1
are initialized, and
\ket{\varphi^{1}}
is:

•

efficiently preparable;

•

key checkable for both
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
and
{\boldsymbol{K}}
;

•

strongly-claw-free for any key pair in
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})
.

Note that we will interchangeably use
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})
or
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in\{{\text{switch}},[0,L]\}}
to denote this tuple of
2+L
output key pairs.

Proof.

The efficiently preparable property comes from the efficiency of
{\mathsf{Adv}}
and the protocol; the key-checkable property comes from the correctness of
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
. We only need to prove the strong claw-freeness.

If this is not true, there will be an index
i\in({\text{switch}})\cup[0,L]
such that an efficient operation
V
can output both keys in
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
given the other keys. Then we can construct an adversary that breaks the claw-free property of
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
as follows:

1.

Instead of interacting with the client, the server simulates all the other
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
evaluations on its own excluding the
i
-th evaluation. In the simulated state the server has access to
(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})
since the client-side key registers for this part are simulated. Denote the simulated state as
\ket{\varphi}
.

2.

Then by the assumption
V
applied on
\ket{\varphi}\odot(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})-{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})

21
21

21

Here we slightly abuse the notation: we use the
\odot
notation on the simulated state to mean that the simulated client side registers are copied to the transcript registers.
outputs both keys in
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
. This contradicts the claw-free property of
{\mathsf{NTCF}}
.

∎

Now we are ready to formalize a
set-up
that abstracts the property of the output state of the first step of Protocol
2
. In the remaining proofs we could only refer to this set-up instead of applying Theorem
6.1
again and again.

Set-up 1
.

We use Setup
1
to denote the set of states that satisfy:

•

The parties are as described in Section
4.1
.

•

The registers are as described in Section
6.1
.

•

The state is efficiently preparable;

•

The state is key checkable for each key in
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})
;

•

The state is strongly-claw-free for each key pair in
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}})
.

6.4
Basis-honest Form

We will define the basis-honest form as below. Recall that in the honest setting, the client holds a tuple of keys, and the server holds superpositions of keys. Correspondingly, in the malicious setting, if the server holds keys in superpositions (which are possibly entangled with some auxiliary registers since it’s malicious), we call this form the basis-honest form.

Let’s first introduce a convenient notation, the
key vector
.

Notation 6.1

(Key vector and subscript vector)
.

Suppose the client holds a tuple of key pairs
\tilde{K}=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
,
K^{(i)}=(x_{0}^{(i)},x_{1}^{(i)}),i\in[L]
. For subscript vector
\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{L}
, denote

\vec{x}_{\vec{b}}:=x^{(1)}_{b^{(1)}}x^{(2)}_{b^{(2)}}\cdots x^{(L)}_{b^{(L)}}

where
b^{(1)}b^{(2)}\cdots b^{(L)}
are coordinates of
\vec{b}
, as the key vector of
\tilde{K}
under subscript vector
\vec{b}
.

If the superscripts of keys start from
0
, the subscript vector will be
\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}
, and the key vector is defined correspondingly as
x^{(0)}_{b^{(0)}}x^{(1)}_{b^{(1)}}x^{(2)}_{b^{(2)}}\cdots x^{(L)}_{b^{(L)}}
.

Definition 6.5

(Basis-honest form)
.

Below we define the basis-honest form of a tuple of key pairs
{\boldsymbol{K}}
(or
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}
), and the basis-honest form of a single key pair. For the first two bullets we use the register setup in Setup
1
.

•

Suppose the client holds a tuple of key pairs in registers
{\boldsymbol{K}}
, and correspondingly the server holds register
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}
. We say a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is in a basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
if it has the form

\ket{\varphi}=\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\underbrace{\ket{K}}_{\text{client-side }{\boldsymbol{K}}}\otimes\sum_{\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}}\underbrace{\ket{\vec{x}_{\vec{b}}}}_{\text{server-side }{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\underbrace{\ket{\varphi_{K,\vec{b}}}}_{\text{remaining registers}}

(101)

We define
\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}
, or simply
\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}
, as the projection onto the subspace that the content of
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}
is a valid key vector of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
.

•

Correspondingly, for key tuple
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
, the basis-honest form is defined to be the state where the server holds a key vector of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}
.
\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}})}
is define similarly.

•

For this bullet we consider a general notion that is not necessarily under Setup
1
. Suppose the client holds a key pair
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
while honestly the server holds a key in
{\boldsymbol{K}}
in register
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}
. Define the basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
to be the states in the form of

\ket{\varphi}=\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\underbrace{\ket{K}}_{\text{client-side }{\boldsymbol{K}}}\otimes\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\underbrace{\ket{x_{b}}}_{\text{server-side }{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\underbrace{\ket{\varphi_{K,b}}}_{\text{remaining registers}}

(102)

And we use
\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}
to denote the projection onto the space that
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}
holds a valid key in
{\boldsymbol{K}}
, and we could omit the register superscript when there is no ambiguity.

Definition 6.6

(Approximate basis-honest form)
.

If
|(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})})\ket{\varphi}|\leq\epsilon
, we say
\ket{\varphi}
is in an
\epsilon
-basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
.

Definition 6.7

(Branch of a basis-honest form)
.

Suppose
\ket{\varphi}
is in a basis-honest form of a key pair
{\boldsymbol{K}}
as shown in (
102
). For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, we call

\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\underbrace{\ket{K}}_{\text{client}}\otimes\underbrace{\ket{x_{b}}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\ket{\varphi_{K,b}}

the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}
-branch of this state.

Suppose
\ket{\varphi}
is in a basis-honest form of a tuple of key pair
{\boldsymbol{K}}
as shown in (
101
). For each
\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}
, we call

\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\underbrace{\ket{K}}_{\text{client}}\otimes\underbrace{\ket{\vec{x}_{\vec{b}}}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\ket{\varphi_{K,\vec{b}}}

the
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}
-branch of this state.

Note when we replace
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[L]}
or
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in\{{\text{switch}},[0,L]\}}
, the definition could be adapted correspondingly.

6.5
{\mathsf{StdBTest}}
Implies Approximate Basis-honest Form

Below we analyze the implications of the standard basis test.
We have the following lemma, which allows us to analyze the structure of a state on the server side assuming it can pass the standard basis test:

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose the client holds a tuple of key pairs in register
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}
, where
D
is a set of indices in Setup
1
. Suppose an efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
, operated on a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
, could pass
{\mathsf{StdBTest}}(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D})
with probability
p
. Then there exists an efficient server-side operation
O
such that
O\ket{\varphi}
is in a
\sqrt{1-p}
-basis-honest form of
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D}
.

Proof.

Run the adversary’s operation but do not do the final measurement. Swap the response register and
({\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)})_{i\in D}
(See Definition
6.5
). The fact that it passes the standard basis test with failure probability
1-p
implies the state on subspace
\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in D})}
has norm at most
\sqrt{1-p}
. ∎

6.6
Useful Lemmas

6.6.1
Collapsing property

One useful lemma is the collapsing property, which is used in different forms in many previous works
[
44
,
35
]
. Intuitively it says if the initial state is claw-free for a key pair then the superposition of two branches is indistinguishable to the mixture of two branches.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose a subnormalized state
\ket{\varphi}
is in Setup
1
, and is in the basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(0)}
. Denote the state of the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{(0)}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(0)}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{1}}
. (That is,
\ket{\varphi_{0}}:=\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}_{0}}\ket{\varphi}
,
\ket{\varphi_{1}}:=\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}_{1}}\ket{\varphi}
.) Then for any efficient server-side operation
O
that output a single bit in a register
{\boldsymbol{S}}
:

|\Pi_{0}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}O\ket{\varphi}|^{2}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}|\Pi_{0}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}O\ket{\varphi_{0}}|^{2}+|\Pi_{0}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}}O\ket{\varphi_{1}}|^{2}

6.6.2
Look-up table encryptions do not affect claw-freeness

Then we give a lemma that formalizes the following intuition. If a basis-honest form state is claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
, if we only consider one branch, for example,
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{0}
-branch, since the server already knows the value of
x^{(i)}_{0}
, it won’t be able to predict
x^{(i)}_{1}
by claw-freeness. This is still true even if the server additionally gets polynomial number of ciphertexts encrypted under
x_{1}^{(i)}
. Formally, we have the following lemma, which will be useful later.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is in Setup
1
and is in a basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
with only the
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b}
-branch, for some
i\in\{{\text{switch}}\}\cup[0,L],b\in\{0,1\}
.
N={\mathsf{poly}}(\kappa)
.
(p_{pre}^{(t)})_{t\in[N]}
is a tuple of strings in
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
,
(p_{post}^{(i)})_{t\in[N]}
is a tuple of strings in
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}\cup\{\emptyset\}
. Define
{\mathsf{AuxInf}}
as the following algorithm that generates a tuple of salted hash values of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1-b}^{(i)}
:

\forall t\in[N]:\text{sample $R^{(t)}\leftarrow_{r}\{0,1\}^{\kappa}$, output }(R^{(t)},H(R^{(t)}||p_{pre}^{(t)}||x_{1-b}^{(i)}),H(R^{(t)}||x_{1-b}^{(i)}||p_{post}^{(t)}))

Then
\ket{\varphi}\odot\llbracket{\mathsf{AuxInf}}\rrbracket
is claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
.

Finally we can show, if some key is unpredictable, the blinded oracle where these entries are blinded looks the same as the original oracle:

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
is in Setup
1
and is in a basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}
with only the
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b}
-branch, for some
b\in\{0,1\}
. suppose
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
is the blinded oracle where
\cdots||{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{1-b}||\cdots
are blinded, where “
\cdots
” represents arbitrary strings of a fixed length. For any efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
, denote
{\mathsf{Adv}}^{\prime}
as the operation that each query in
{\mathsf{Adv}}
is replaced by a query to
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
. Then

{\mathsf{Adv}}\ket{\varphi}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}{\mathsf{Adv}}^{\prime}\ket{\varphi}

We put the proofs to these lemmas in Appendix
B
.

6.6.3
Rigidity of basis-honest form with strong-claw-free condition

We show a useful lemma that will be used for multiple times in later proofs. It says, if a state is in a basis-honest form with good security properties, if the adversary transforms it to another basis-honest form, each of the outcome branch should only come from the corresponding input branch:

Lemma 6.6
.

Consider a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
in Setup
1
. Then for any efficient server-side operation
D
there is

\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}D\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\sum_{\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}}\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}D\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\ket{\varphi}

(103)

where
\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}
denotes the projection onto
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}
-branch (Definition
6.7
).

Proof.

\forall i\in[0,L]
define:

\ket{\varphi^{i}}=\sum_{b^{(0)}b^{(1)}\cdots b^{(i)}\in\{0,1\}^{1+i}}\Pi_{b^{(0)}b^{(1)}\cdots b^{(i)}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}D\Pi_{b^{(0)}b^{(1)}\cdots b^{(i)}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

where
\Pi_{b^{(0)}b^{(1)}\cdots b^{(i)}}
denotes the projection onto the space that the values of
{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}^{(i)}_{bsh}}
are equal to the values of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b^{(0)}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b^{(1)}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b^{(i)}}^{(i)}
.

Additionally define

\ket{\varphi^{-1}}=\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}D\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

To prove (
140
) we only need to prove:

\forall i\in[0,L],\ket{\varphi^{i}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\ket{\varphi^{i-1}}

(104)

The observation is, although
\ket{\varphi^{i-1}},\ket{\varphi^{i}}
are written as linear sums, there exists an efficient operation that exactly prepares this state from
\ket{\varphi}
. Recall
\ket{\varphi}
has the form shown in (
101
). Let’s give an operation that transforms it to
\ket{\varphi^{i}}
.

Initialize server-side auxiliary registers
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}
, and define the following operations:

•

Define
{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i}
as the operation that copies (bit-wise
{\sf CNOT}
) the contents of
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}
to registers
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}
. Define
{\sf COPY}_{i}
as the operator that only copies
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}
to
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}
.

•

Define
\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}}
as the operator that projects onto the space that the values of
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}
are equal to
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}
. Define
\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}}
as the operator that projects onto the space that
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}
is equal to
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}
.

Then:

\ket{\varphi^{i}}=\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i}\circ\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}}D\circ{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i}\circ\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

Similarly

\ket{\varphi^{i-1}}=\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i-1}\circ\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i-1)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i-1)}}D\circ{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i-1}\circ\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

Define
{\mathcal{P}}={\sf COPY}_{0\sim i-1}\circ\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i-1)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(0)}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(1)}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i-1)}}D\circ{\sf COPY}_{0\sim i-1}
. Then (
104
) is reduced to proving

\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}{\mathcal{P}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}{\sf COPY}_{i}\circ\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}={\boldsymbol{A}}^{(i)}}\circ{\mathcal{P}}\circ{\sf COPY}_{i}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

(105)

Denote
\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}
as the operator that projects onto the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}
-branch of the basis-honest form. Then the left hand side of (
105
) is

\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}{\mathcal{P}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}=\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b^{\prime}}^{(i)}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}{\mathcal{P}}\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

and the right hand side of (
105
) is

\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}{\mathcal{P}}\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}^{(i)}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}

then (
105
) is further reduced to

\sum_{b,b^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2},b\neq b^{\prime}}\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b^{\prime}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}\circ{\mathcal{P}}\circ\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(i)}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}0

(106)

which holds by the claw-free property of
\ket{\varphi}
.∎

7
The Switch Gadget Technique

In this section we analyze the switch gadget technique and how it affects the security properties of Protocol
2
. We have discussed the switch gadget technique briefly in the introduction; below we will review it and discuss its formal analysis. The basic lemma behind the switch gadget technique is given in Section
7.1
, based on the notion of
blinded oracle
. In Section
7.2
we give several basic lemmas on RO-padded Hadamard test. The basic lemma behind the switch gadget technique is proved in Section
7.3
.

As discussed in the introduction, in the switch gadget technique we design protocols that go as follows:

1.

Encode a mapping onto a switch gadget;

2.

Do a Hadamard test (Protocol
7
) on the switch gadget. This destroys the switch gadget.

The switch gadget is used as a “switch” that controls whether the adversary could make use of the mapping. With the switch gadget, the honest server could implement the mapping; the problem is how to characterize the adversaries’ view, assuming it wants to pass the Hadamard test with high probability.

An intuitive discussion on Hadamard test

We need to formalize the intuition that

K
is a key pair, and the initial state is claw-free for
K
. If the server wants to pass the RO-padded Hadamard test for
K
, it loses the keys after the test.

As
[
61
]
pointed out, passing the RO-padded Hadamard test implies the server could not do powerful things about
K
from the post-test state. However the lemmas given in
[
61
]
are not suitable for our purpose here. Here we give a lemma that captures the properties of RO-padded Hadamard test in terms of
blinded oracle
(Section
3.3
). Our analysis of the RO-padded Hadamard test could also be of independent interest elsewhere.

Suppose the Hadamard test is on key pair
K^{({\text{switch}})}
and initial state
\ket{\varphi}
. Suppose the post-test state is
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
. To formalize the intuition above, we consider a
blinded oracle

H^{\prime}
where the entries in the form of
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Then we will show, informally, if the test passes with high probability:

Starting from
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
, for any efficient adversary, querying
H
and querying
H^{\prime}
should end up with similar states.

The formal statement goes roughly as follows. For any efficient operator
D
on the post-test state, define
D^{\prime}
as the blinded version of
D
where all the random oracle queries in
D
are replaced by queries to
H^{\prime}
, there is

D^{\prime}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\approx D\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}

(107)

How is (
107
) related to the intuition above? If the server could still predict a key in
K
from the post-test state, it can query the oracle with this key and make two sides of (
107
) quite different. Thus (
107
) describes (and strengthens) the intuition above.

This statement gives what we want from the switch gadget technique: the ciphertexts within our lookup tables (which encrypt the phases) are encrypted under
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
; and after applying this lemma, we only need to analyze a blinded oracle where this form of entries are blinded, which means the phase information encrypted under these lookup tables will become secret again.

The formal theorem is given below in Theorem
7.1
.

7.1
Basic Lemma Behind the Switch Gadget Technique

We first formalize the set-up of the theorem as follows. We note in this subsection we do not follow the register symbols in Section
6.1
; specifically,
{\boldsymbol{K}}
will be used to denote a key pair, to make our lemmas more general.

Set-up 2
.

Suppose the parties are formalized in Section
4.1
. Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
. Define
{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}})
as the set of purified joint states that are

•

efficiently preparable;

•

key checkable for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
;

•

claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
.

In addition, a transcript register
{\boldsymbol{d}}
is initialized, whose length is equal to the length of keys in
{\boldsymbol{K}}
plus
\kappa
(security parameter).

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
. Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}\in{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})})
is in the
\epsilon
-basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
. For any polynomial time adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
, denote the post-execution state of the RO-padded Hadamard test as

\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}={\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}

Then at least one of the following two is true:

•

(Small passing probability)

|\Pi_{{\mathsf{pass}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|^{2}\leq 1-p

•

(RO hiding) Suppose
H^{\prime}
is the blinded oracle where the entries
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. For any efficient operation
D
, suppose
D^{\prime}
is the blinded version of
D
where all the oracle queries are replaced by queries to
H^{\prime}
. Then

D^{\prime}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\approx_{8\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}

(108)

To prove this theorem, we need to analyze the RO-padded Hadamard test. We first prove some basic lemmas on this test. Note these lemmas will also be independently useful in later proofs.

7.2
Analysis of RO-padded Hadamard test

We first introduce the following notations which explicitly describe the passing/failing and winning/losing conditions of these Hadamard test. Below we will use these notations to describe the output of Hadamard test.

Notation 7.1
.

Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
. Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}\in{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}})
. Recall that in
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}
the client first sample a random padding in
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
, denote the register storing it as
{\boldsymbol{pad}}
. Define the following operators on system
{\boldsymbol{d}}
(introduced in
{\text{SETUP}}^{2}
):

•

\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}
denotes the projection onto the space that the values of client side registers
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}
, the transcript registers
{\boldsymbol{d}}
,
{\boldsymbol{pad}}
, and the random oracle registers satisfy: the result of calculation of (
79
) is
0
:

d\cdot(x_{0}||\underbrace{H(pad||x_{0})}_{\text{$\kappa$ bits}})+d\cdot(x_{1}||H(pad||x_{1}))=0

•

\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}
is defined similarly.

•

\Pi_{=0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}
is the projection onto the space that the last
\kappa
bits of
{\boldsymbol{d}}
is all zero.
\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}
is defined as its complement. Note this is one of the client’s checking in
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}
.

The following lemma studies the output property of
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}
on a single branch:

Lemma 7.2
.

Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
. Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}\in{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}})
and is in the basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
with only
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}
branch. Then for any efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
there is

|\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}|\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}|

|\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}|\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}|

Then we have the following corollary, which studies the relations of two branches if a state could pass the
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}
:

Corollary 7.3
.

Suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1})
. Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}\in{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}})
and is in an
\epsilon
-basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}
. Denote the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{1}}
. If an efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
could make the client output
{\mathsf{pass}}
in the Hadamard test (Protocol
5
) from initial state
\ket{\varphi}
with probability
\geq 1-p
, then

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(p+2\epsilon)}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}}

(109)

\Pi_{=0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}0,

\Pi_{=0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}0

(110)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}

\displaystyle-\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}}

(111)

These lemmas are proved in Appendix
C
.

7.3
Proof of Theorem
7.1

Now we give the proof of Theorem
7.1
with this lemma.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Suppose
|\Pi_{{\mathsf{pass}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|^{2}\geq 1-p
. Denote the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{({\text{switch}})}
-branch of
\ket{\varphi}
as
\ket{\varphi_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{({\text{switch}})}
-branch of
\ket{\varphi}
as
\ket{\varphi_{1}}
. Then
\ket{\varphi}\approx_{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})})}\ket{\varphi}=\ket{\varphi_{0}}+\ket{\varphi_{1}}
. Define the corresponding output term:

\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}={\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}}

\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}={\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}}

Then

\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\approx_{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}+\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}

(112)

We will blind the oracle in two steps.

1.

Define
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{mid}
as the blinded oracle where the entries
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||x_{0}^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Define
D^{mid}
as the adversary where all the random oracle queries in
D
are replaced by queries to
H^{mid}
. Our goal is to prove

D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\approx_{4\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}

(113)

The reason is as follows.

We will first analyze the effect of replacing
D
by
D^{mid}
on
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}
. Intuitively
\ket{\varphi_{1}}
is the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{({\text{switch}})}
-branch, the server could not predict
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{({\text{switch}})}
by claw-freeness thus the blinding operation on
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{({\text{switch}})}
-related entries will not be detected. Formally speaking, the following is implied by Lemma
6.5
:

D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}

(114)

Then since the
{\boldsymbol{d}}
register, after generated in the Hadamard test, is read-only, both sides of (
114
) are still close to each other if a projection on a subset of possible values of
{\boldsymbol{d}}
is applied. Concretely:

D^{mid}\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}},

(115)

D^{mid}\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}

(116)

Now we use properties of the Hadamard test to argue about the
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}
branch. By Corollary
7.3
we have

\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}\approx_{\sqrt{2(p+2\epsilon)}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}

(117)

\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}-\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}

(118)

which together with (
115
) (
116
) implies

D^{mid}\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}\approx_{\sqrt{2(p+2\epsilon)}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}},

(119)

D^{mid}\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}

(120)

which together with (
110
) implies

D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}\approx_{2.5\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}

(121)

which together with (
114
)(
112
) implies (
113
).

2.

Now we hide the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{({\text{switch}})}
part using similar techniques. Consider
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
as the blinded oracle of
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{mid}
where the entries
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||x_{1}^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Then
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
could also be seen as a blinded version of
{\boldsymbol{H}}
where
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Correspondingly,
D^{\prime}
is defined to be: in
D^{mid}
all the queries to
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{mid}
are replaced by queries to
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
. Then
D^{\prime}
is also the blinded version of
D
where all the queries to
{\boldsymbol{H}}
are replaced by queries to
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
. Our goal is to prove

D^{\prime}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}\approx_{4\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}

(122)

Here we will start with the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{({\text{switch}})}
branch of
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
6.5
there is

D^{\prime}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}

(123)

Then arguments (
115
)-(
121
) hold after all the appearances of
D
in them are replaced by
D^{\prime}
,
\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}
are replaced by
\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
and
\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
are replaced by
\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}
. Thus we get

D^{mid}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}\approx_{2.5\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}D^{\prime}\ket{\varphi^{\prime}_{1}}

(124)

Combining it with (
123
) completes the proof of (
122
).

Now combining (
113
)(
122
) completes the proof.
∎

8
Analysis of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
, the Switch Gadget Technique Part

Now we return to the
{\mathsf{preRSPV}}
protocol and see how the switch gadget technique is used to argue about the security.

8.1
Switch Gadget Technique Implies the Output Closeness of Original Adversary and Blinded Adversary in Later Steps

In the following theorem, we use the switch gadget technique theorem to study the behavior of the output state of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
in later steps. We will see, in later steps, we can replace the adversary by its blinded version up to an approximation.

Theorem 8.1
.

Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
in Setup
1
and is in an
\epsilon
-basis-honest form for
{\boldsymbol{K}}
. For any polynomial-time adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
, suppose the state after the
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
step is
\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}
:

\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}={\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}_{2.a}}(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in({\text{switch}})\cup[0,L]},{\boldsymbol{\Theta}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi}

where
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{2.a}
is the part of adversary in
{\mathsf{Adv}}
in the
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
step. Suppose

|\Pi_{{\mathsf{pass}}}\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}|^{2}\geq 1-p.

(125)

Use
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
to denote the blinded version of
{\boldsymbol{H}}
where the entries
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Denote
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}^{blind}
as the adversary where all the oracle queries in
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}
are replaced by queries to
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
. Then we have

{\mathsf{preRSPV}}_{\geq 2.b}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}}\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}\approx_{8\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}{\mathsf{preRSPV}}_{\geq 2.b}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}^{blind}}\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}

(126)

The difference of this theorem from Theorem
7.1
is the client might send additional messages to the server. Notice that the client-side messages in
{\mathsf{preRSPV}}_{\geq 2.b}
do not take
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
as inputs, we are able to reduce this theorem to Theorem
7.1
by constructing an adversary that simulates these messages.

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Note that in each step of
{\mathsf{preRSPV}}_{\geq 2.b}
, the client’s messages are output of some algorithms which take
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
as inputs, and
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
is not used any more. Consider an adversary
({\mathsf{Adv}}_{2.a},{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b})
that violates (
126
). We can construct an adversary for breaking Theorem
7.1
as follows:

0.

The initial state is

\ket{\varphi}\odot({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}\odot{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\odot\llbracket{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}_{1}(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in({\text{switch}})\cup[0,L]},{\boldsymbol{\Theta}};1^{\kappa})\rrbracket

(127)

where
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}_{1}
is the first step in
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
(where the client sends lookup tables that encode the phases).

By Lemma
6.4
we know (
127
) is claw-free for
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})}
.

1.

The adversary executes
{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})};1^{\kappa})
with the client. It runs the code of
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{2.a}
in this step.

2.

The adversary simulates all the client side messages in
{\mathsf{preRSPV}}_{\geq 2.b}
using
({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
.
Run
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}
with the simulated messages.

(
127
) satisfies the conditions required in Theorem
7.1
. Since (
125
) holds we know the first case in Theorem
7.1
is not true. Then the violation of (
126
) implies a violation of the second case in Theorem
7.1
, where
D
,
D^{\prime}
in Theorem
7.1
translate to:

•

D
corresponds to
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}
run on simulated messages.

•

D^{\prime}
corresponds to
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{\geq 2.b}^{blind}
run on simulated messages.

This completes the proof.∎

Implication for later proofs

The implication of Theorem
8.1
is, in the protocol analysis later, we can first use this theorem to replace the adversary by an adversary that only queries the blinded oracle. Especially, the phase information used in
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
is encrypted under the switch gadget keys, blinding this part of the oracle implies the secrecy of phase information in later steps.

8.2
Set-up for the Output State of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}

In this section we formalize a set-up that captures the basic properties of the output states of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
. In the later proofs when we need to further analyze the output state of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
we could simply refer to this set-up.

Set-up 3
.

Setup
3
is defined as the set of states that could be expressed as

{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}(({\boldsymbol{K}}^{({\text{switch}})},{\boldsymbol{K}}),{\boldsymbol{\Theta}};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi^{1}},

(128)

where
{\mathsf{Adv}}
is efficient,
\ket{\varphi^{1}}
is in Setup
1
.

Accompanied with Setup
3
, we introduce the following symbols for registers and describe the property of states in Setup
3
:

Notation 8.1
.

We introduce the following notations that describe the transcript registers initialized in the first step of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
:

The client-side messages in the first step of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
contain ciphertexts that encrypt
\theta^{(i)}_{b}
under
x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)}
for each
i\in[0,L],b\in\{0,1\},b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\}
. In the protocol it is denoted as

x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)}\rightarrow\theta_{b}^{(i)}

(129)

Recall in Definition
3.7
,
3.6
, the ciphertext part of (
129
) is defined as

R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b},H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})+\theta_{b}^{(i)}

(130)

where
R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}
is uniformly sampled from
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}
.

Denote the transcript registers that store (
130
) during the protocol by
{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b},{\boldsymbol{ct}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}
correspondingly. Then related registers for (
130
) are:

\underbrace{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}}_{\text{client}},\underbrace{{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x^{(i)}_{b})}_{\text{random oracle}},\underbrace{{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b},{\boldsymbol{ct}}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}^{(i)}}_{\text{transcript}}

(131)

and there is (recall Notation
3.3
):

{\boldsymbol{ct}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}={\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x^{(i)}_{b})+{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}

(132)

In summary, for any state in Setup
3
, for any
i\in[0,L]
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\}
, there are registers shown in (
131
) and their values satisfy (
132
).

We also introduce the following blinded oracle that accompanies Setup
3
:

Notation 8.2
.

Under Setup
3
, define
{\boldsymbol{H}}^{\prime}
as the blinded oracle where entries in the form of
\{0,1\}^{\kappa}||K^{({\text{switch}})}||\cdots
are blinded. Define
{\mathcal{F}}_{blind}
as the set of server-side operators that could only query this blinded oracle.

8.3
Preparation for the Later Proofs: De-correlate the
{\boldsymbol{H}}
Registers by
{\sf ReviseRO}
Operator

We introduce an operation as a preparation for later proofs. Recall that in Example
2.1
we discuss the randomization operators that operate on the client side phase registers and keep the state approximately invariant; but the state that we use in the example has an important difference from the outcome of the real protocol (characterized by Setup
3
). In a state in Setup
3
, the messages of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
are stored in the transcript registers, and the server also has access to it. Taking this into consideration, the purified joint state of both parties is generally described as

\forall i\in[0,L],b\in\{0,1\},b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\},\quad\underbrace{\ket{H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})}}_{{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})}\underbrace{\ket{\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}}\underbrace{\ket{H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})+\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{ct}}^{(0)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}}

(133)

together with other registers. Taking these ciphertexts and the related registers into consideration, the swapping operator that swaps
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
with freshly new randomness (as discussed in the example) does not keep the state invariant.
22
22

22

Note after the swapping the random oracle content,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
register, and the
{\boldsymbol{ct}}
register does not necessarily satisfy Equation (
132
) given in Setup
3
.
We introduce the following operation to make this swap-based randomization work again. This operator will erase the content of the
{\boldsymbol{H}}
registers, and thus de-correlate this register with the other parts; this operator will significantly change the state, but we can show, the original state and the new state are indistinguishable under the family of distinguishers that we care about.

Definition 8.1

(
{\sf ReviseRO}
)
.

Under Setup
3
, for each
i\in[0,L],b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\},b\in\{0,1\}
, the
{\sf ReviseRO}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}
operator is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\underbrace{\ket{H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})}}_{{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})}\underbrace{\ket{\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}}\underbrace{\ket{H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})+\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{ct}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}}

(134)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\sf ReviseRO}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\ket{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})}\underbrace{\ket{\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}}\underbrace{\ket{H(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})+\theta_{b}^{(i)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{ct}}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}}

(135)

Then define
{\sf ReviseRO}
to be the following operation:

1.

For each
i\in[0,L],b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\},b\in\{0,1\}
:

(a)

Apply
{\sf ReviseRO}^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}
.

(b)

Apply Hadamard gates on each bit of RO register
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})
.

The second step above is to re-create uniformly random coins for the random oracle, and thus preserve the validity of the state in the random oracle model (Definition
3.4
).

Fact 8
.

Suppose
\ket{\varphi}
is in Setup
3
. Then
{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi}
is a valid state in QROM.

Proof.

We only need to prove for each
i\in[0,L],b^{({\text{switch}})}\in\{0,1\},b\in\{0,1\}
, the corresponding operation in the construction preserves the validity of the state. Suppose for the original state
\ket{\varphi}
when
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})=h
, the state in registers excluding
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})
is
\ket{\varphi_{h}}
. Then
\ket{\varphi_{h}}
for different
h
is orthogonal to each other by (
132
). By the construction of
{\sf ReviseRO}
, in
{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi}
, when
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})=h
, the state in registers excluding
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})
is a superposition of each
\ket{\varphi_{h}}
with different phases. Then since
\ket{\varphi}
is valid the new state is also valid.
∎

Below we show the application of
{\sf ReviseRO}
keeps the state indistinguishable under a class of operations that is sufficiently big to cover the distinguishers that we care about in the main proof.

Definition 8.2
.

{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}
is defined to be the set of operators that take the client-side
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
registers and the transcript registers read-only and only query the blinded oracle.

The requirement “take
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
read-only” corresponds to the client-side read-only requirement on the distinguisher in Definition
4.3
. What’s more,
{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{blind}
.

Fact 9
.

Suppose
\ket{\varphi}
is in Setup
3
. Then
{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi}\approx^{ind:{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}}\ket{\varphi}
.

Proof.

By Fact
1
both
\ket{\varphi}
and
{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi}
are indistinguishable under
{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}
to a state where
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
,
{\boldsymbol{ct}}
registers are all cloned to the environment. Then
{\sf ReviseRO}
becomes an operation that only operates on the registers that
{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}
never uses thus keeps the state indistinguishable.
∎

8.4
Outcome of
{\sf ReviseRO}
is Almost Efficiently-preparable

In the subsections above we introduce the
{\sf ReviseRO}
operators, and show the output state from the protocol remains indistinguishable under
{\mathcal{F}}_{cq\land blind}
. But there is still one more thing to worry about: the output of
{\sf ReviseRO}
is not obviously efficiently-preparable, since it revises the random oracle registers in a way that is not allowed in the definition of efficient-preparation. Below we will show the output state is still efficiently preparable up to an exponentially small error, and show many lemmas that we need still hold for this type of states.

8.4.1
Approximate efficient preparation of the output state

Recall that a state
\ket{\varphi}
in Setup
3
could be written as

\displaystyle\ket{\varphi}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}}\ket{\varphi^{1}}

(136)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{PadHadamard}}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}}(\ket{\varphi^{1}}\odot\llbracket{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{1}\rrbracket)

(137)

where
\ket{\varphi^{1}}
is in Setup
1
,
\llbracket{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{1}\rrbracket
is the client-side messages in the first step of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
(that is, the lookup tables).

Suppose the set of random padding in
\llbracket{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{1}\rrbracket
is
R
. By Lemma
3.4
we have, there exists
\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}
that is independent to
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R||\cdots)
, and
\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\ket{\varphi^{1}}
.

Now we claim the state

{\sf ReviseRO}\circ{\mathsf{PadHadamard}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}(\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}\odot\llbracket{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}^{1}\rrbracket)

(138)

is efficiently-preparable. We give the following construction.

1.

Starting from
\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}
, fill uniform superpositions in all the registers in
{\boldsymbol{ct}}
.

2.

Run
{\mathsf{PadHadamard}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}
, and for each query to
H
, replace it by the following operation: if the input has the form of
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R^{(i)}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}||x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x_{b}^{(i)})
, use
{\boldsymbol{ct}}_{b^{({\text{switch}})},b}^{(i)}-{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{b}
as the query outcome; otherwise use the corresponding output of
H
.

The reason is as follows. Starting from (
138
), first since in
{\mathsf{PadHadamard}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}
registers
{\boldsymbol{ct}},{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
are read-only, and the
{\boldsymbol{H}}
registers,
{\boldsymbol{ct}}
registers and
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
registers satisfy (
132
), we can replace each query by the construction in the second step above, and the state does not change. Then since
\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}
does not depend on
{\boldsymbol{H}}(R||\cdots)
,
{\sf ReviseRO}
together with the preparation of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
commutes with the preparation of
\ket{\tilde{\varphi}^{1}}
and
{\mathsf{PadHadamard}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}
. Then a direct calculation of
{\sf ReviseRO}
on the superpositions of all the basis components that satisfy (
132
) shows the output of this operator is the uniform superposition in all the
{\boldsymbol{ct}}
registers and
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
registers.

8.4.2
A list of useful lemmas

The discussions above imply that, many lemmas that we proved before under the efficiently-preparable property, still hold for states in the form of

{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\phi},\ket{\phi}\in\text{ Setup \ref{setup:3}}

(139)

Note that when we construct the efficient-preparable operator that approximately prepare (
139
) the operator also operates on the client-side register
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
to simulate operators that are originally solely server-side. But we can only focus on lemmas that remain true even if all these
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
registers are considered server-side registers. We list the following lemmas that will be used in later proofs.

Lemma 8.2

(Analog of Lemma
6.6
)
.

Consider a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
in (
139
). Suppose
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}={\mathsf{Prtl}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}\ket{\varphi}
where the protocol
{\mathsf{Prtl}}
and adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}
are both efficient and do not query the blinded part of
H
.

Use
\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}

to denote the projection onto
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}
-branch (Definition
6.7
). Then for any efficient server-side operation
D\in{\mathcal{F}}_{blind}
there is

\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}D\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\sum_{\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{L}}\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}D\Pi_{\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}}^{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}}\ket{\varphi}

(140)

The two lemmas below are analog of Lemma
7.2
and Corollary
7.3
, and we use
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime}
to replace
{\boldsymbol{K}}
in these lemmas since the symbol
{\boldsymbol{K}}
is occupied by the key tuple appeared in the protocol.

Lemma 8.3

(Analog of Lemma
7.2
)
.

Consider a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
in (
139
). Suppose
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}={\mathsf{Prtl}}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}_{0}}\ket{\varphi}
where the protocol
{\mathsf{Prtl}}
and adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{0}
are both efficient and do not query the blinded part of
H
. Then suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\prime})
, and
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
satisfies all the conditions in
{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime})
except the first bullet, and is in the basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime}
with only
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
branch. Then for any efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{blind}
there is

|\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|

|\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}|

Corollary 8.4

(Analog of Corollary
7.3
)
.

Consider a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
in (
139
). Suppose
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}={\mathsf{Prtl}}^{{\mathsf{Adv}}_{0}}\ket{\varphi}
where the protocol
{\mathsf{Prtl}}
and adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}_{0}
are both efficient and do not query the blinded part of
H
. Then suppose the client holds a key pair in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\prime})
, and
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
satisfies all the conditions in
{\text{SETUP}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime})
except the first bullet, and is in an
\epsilon
-basis-honest form of
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime}
. Denote the
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{0}^{\prime}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\prime}
-branch as
\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}
. If an efficient adversary
{\mathsf{Adv}}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{blind}
could make the client output
{\mathsf{pass}}
in the Hadamard test (Protocol
5
) from initial state
\ket{\varphi^{\prime}}
with probability
\geq 1-p
, then

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{2(p+2\epsilon)}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=0}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}

(141)

\Pi_{=0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}0,

\Pi_{=0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}\approx_{\sqrt{p+2\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}0

(142)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{0}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\approx_{\sqrt{p+\epsilon}+{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}

\displaystyle-\Pi_{\eqref{eq:htt}=1}^{{\boldsymbol{d}}}\Pi_{\neq 0}^{\text{last $\kappa$ bits of }{\boldsymbol{d}}}{\mathsf{HadamardTest}}^{\mathsf{Adv}}({\boldsymbol{K}}^{\prime};1^{\kappa})\ket{\varphi_{1}^{\prime}}

(143)

We put their proofs in Appendix
D
.

9
Analysis of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
, the Lookup-table Part

As described in the introduction, we will prove the joint state of the client and the server remain indistinguishable under a series of randomization operators.

In this section, we will focus on what the
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
protocol itself could tell us. The analysis will be based on the structure of phase tables.

Recall that each row of the phase tables used in this protocol has the following structure:

x^{({\text{switch}})}_{b^{({\text{switch}})}}||x^{(i)}_{b}\rightarrow\theta^{(i)}_{b};\quad i\in[0,L],b^{({\text{switch}})},b\in\{0,1\}

(144)

Intuitively, the theorems that we prove in this section is based on the following intuition: if the server does not hold
x_{b}^{(i)}
, it could not decrypt (
144
), thus
\theta^{(i)}_{b}
remains secure. (Recall by the claw-freeness the server does not know
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{b}
on the
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{1-b}
-branch, and
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}
is the client-side register that stores the corresponding phase information.)
Furthermore, for each
\vec{b}=b^{(0)}b^{(1)}\cdots b^{(L)}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}
, on the
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}
-branch, the server holds
x^{(0)}_{b^{(0)}}x^{(1)}_{b^{(1)}}\cdots x^{(L)}_{b^{(L)}}
but does not know
x^{(0)}_{1-b^{(0)}}x^{(1)}_{1-b^{(1)}}\cdots x^{(L)}_{1-b^{(L)}}
. Thus intuitively:

On the
\vec{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\vec{b}}
-branch, the server-side state should not depend on the values of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(0)}_{1-b^{(0)}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(1)}_{1-b^{(1)}}\cdots{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(L)}_{1-b^{(L)}}
.

In this section we build the bridge between this intuition and the protocol as follows.

•

In Section
9.1
we define the
basis-phase correspondence form
, which characterizes the states that perfectly satisfy the intuition above.

•

In Section
9.2
we construct a randomization operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
, which transforms a general basis-honest form to a basis-phase-correspondence form.

•

In Section
9.3
we show the output states of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
are approximately invariant under
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
.

•

Finally in Section
9.4
we formalize a new setup for the outcome of
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
, which will be used in later analysis.

9.1
Basis-phase Correspondence Form

As discussed in the technical overview, we define the
basis-phase correspondence form
as follows.

Definition 9.1
.

Assume the parties are as in Section
4.1
and the registers are as in Section
6.1
. We say a state
\ket{\varphi}
is in a basis-phase correspondence form if there exists states
\ket{\varphi_{K,\vec{b},\vec{\theta}}}
for each
K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})
,
\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}
,
\vec{\theta}\in\{0,1\cdots 7\}^{1+L}
such that

\ket{\varphi}=\underbrace{\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{K}\otimes\sum_{\Theta\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{\Theta}})}\ket{\Theta}}_{\text{client}}\otimes\underbrace{\sum_{\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}}\ket{\vec{x}_{\vec{b}}}}_{\text{server-side register ${\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}$}}\otimes\ket{\varphi_{K,\vec{b},\vec{\Theta}_{\vec{b}}}}

Recall
\vec{\Theta}_{\vec{b}}
denotes
\theta^{(0)}_{b^{(0)}}\theta^{(1)}_{b^{(1)}}\cdots\theta^{(L)}_{b^{(L)}}
.

9.2
Randomization operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}

In this subsection we define the randomization operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
.

9.2.1
Intuitive discussion

Example 9.1
.

First we can recall that an intuitive introduction of the
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
operator is given in Example
2.1
. Considering the
x_{0}
-branch in the example, if we omit the unused registers, Equation (
50
) in the example becomes

\ket{\Delta_{1}}\ket{\theta_{1}}\ket{x_{0}}\rightarrow\ket{\theta_{1}}\ket{\Delta_{1}}\ket{x_{0}}

(145)

Under Setup
3
, considering the
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(0)}_{0}
-branch as an example, (
145
) becomes

\ket{\Delta_{1}^{(0)}}\underbrace{\ket{\theta_{1}^{(0)}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(0)}_{0}}\underbrace{\ket{x_{0}^{(0)}}}_{\text{server-side register ${\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}^{(0)}$}}\rightarrow\ket{\theta_{1}^{(0)}}\ket{\Delta_{1}^{(0)}}\ket{x_{0}^{(0)}}

(146)

We will see
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
is defined to be this type of operations applied on each possible superscript in
[0,L]
and each possible subscript in
\{0,1\}
.

9.2.2
Formal definition

To formalize this operator, let’s define an operator that operates on a
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{1-b}
branch of a basis-honest state, and randomizes the phase information register
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{b}^{(i)}
by swapping it with a completely new random value
\Delta^{(i)}_{b}
:

Definition 9.2
.

Recall in Setup
3
the client holds a tuple of key pairs in register
{\boldsymbol{K}}=({\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
,
{\boldsymbol{K}}^{(i)}=({\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{b})_{b\in\{0,1\}}
. And the client additionally holds a tuple of phase pairs in register
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=({\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]}
,
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{(i)}=({\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{b})_{b\in\{0,1\}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{b}\in\{0,1\cdots 7\}
.
For a purified joint state
\ket{\varphi}
, expand the basis-honest part:

\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{\varphi}=\underbrace{\sum_{K\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}\ket{K}\otimes\sum_{\Theta\in{\text{Domain}}({\boldsymbol{\Theta}})}\ket{\Theta}}_{\text{client}}\otimes\underbrace{\sum_{\vec{b}\in\{0,1\}^{1+L}}\ket{\vec{x}_{\vec{b}}}}_{\text{server-side register ${\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}$}}\otimes\ket{\varphi_{K,\Theta,\vec{b}}}

(147)

For any
i\in[0,L],b\in\{0,1\}
, define
{\sf SWAP}_{1-b,b}^{(i)}
as follows. First initialize randomness register
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{(i)}_{b}
to hold uniformly distributed value
\in\{0,1\cdots 7\}
. Then
{\sf SWAP}_{1-b,b}^{(i)}
is the following control-swap operation that acts nontrivially on the
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(i)}_{1-b}
branch of (
147
) and swaps the value of
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{b}
with the value of
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{b}^{(i)}
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\ket{\Delta^{(i)}_{b}}}_{{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{(i)}_{b}}\underbrace{\ket{K}\ket{\cdots,\underbrace{\theta^{(i)}_{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{b}},\cdots}}_{\text{client}}\otimes\underbrace{\ket{\cdots x^{(i)}_{1-b}\cdots}}_{\text{server-side register ${\boldsymbol{S}}_{bsh}$}}\quad\quad(K=(K^{(i)})_{i\in[0,L]},K^{(i)}=(x^{(i)}_{b})_{b\in\{0,1\}})

(148)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\sf SWAP}_{1-b,b}^{(i)}}

\displaystyle\ket{\theta_{b}^{(i)}}\ket{K}\ket{\cdots,\Delta^{(i)}_{b},\cdots}\otimes\ket{\cdots x^{(i)}_{1-b}\cdots}

(149)

The operator acts as identity on the other branch and outside
\Pi_{{\text{basishonest}}({\boldsymbol{K}})}
.

Then the randomization operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
is to apply
{\sf SWAP}^{(i)}_{1-b,b}
for all the possible
i,b
:

Definition 9.3
.

Consider the same register set-up as Definition
9.2
. Additionally introduce registers
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}^{(i)}_{b}
,
i\in[0,L],b\in\{0,1\}
, which are initialized to hold the state

\ket{\$_{1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{8^{2(L+1)}}}\sum_{\forall i\in[0,L],\forall b\in\{0,1\}:\Delta^{(i)}_{b}\in\{0,1\cdots 7\}}\ket{(\underbrace{\Delta^{(i)}_{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{b}^{(i)}})_{i\in[0,L],b\in\{0,1\}}}

Define randomization operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
as

\circ_{\forall i\in[0,L],\forall b\in\{0,1\}}{\sf SWAP}^{(i)}_{1-b,b}

Note that (1) for each
i,b
the
{\sf SWAP}
operator uses freshly new randomness; (2)
\circ
denote the operator composition; since these
{\sf SWAP}
operators commute with each other the order of applying these
{\sf SWAP}
operators does not matter; (3) when we say “randomizing a state
\ket{\varphi}
with
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
”, we mean applying
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
on
\ket{\$_{1}}\otimes\ket{\varphi}
.

We will show:

•

Applying
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
to the output of
{\mathsf{SwPhaseUpdate}}
keeps the state indistinguishable;

•

Applying
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
takes the state to a class of states that satisfy specific properties.

9.3
Phase Table Structure Implies Approximate Invariance Under Randomization of
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}

The following theorem says the application of
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}
keeps the state approximately invariant.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose a sub-normalized purified joint state
\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}
is in Setup
3
. Then

{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(\ket{\$_{1}}\otimes{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi^{2.a}})\approx_{{\mathsf{negl}}(\kappa)}{\sf ReviseRO}\ket{\varphi^{2.a}}

(150)

9.3.1
Linear algebra fact that connects state form to approximate-invariance of operator

To prove this theorem, we give the following linear algebra fact that says, if the state is close to a specific form, it is approximate-invariant under a randomization operator.

Fact 10
.

Suppose
\ket{\varphi}
satisfies:
